{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5-5","id":"msg_011CfkVKCkyTCQEP2nNYVEk4","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"The nbody benchmark now runs as MPI + OpenMP + CUDA, with changes only in `nbody.cpp` and `CMakeLists.txt`. Its output is bit-for-bit identical to the original program's. The executable is still `nbody` and is launched with `mpirun -np <ranks> ./nbody ...`.\n\n**How the work is split**\n- **MPI:** bodies are divided into equal contiguous blocks, one rank per GPU. Each rank picks its GPU from its position among the ranks on its node. After every step the ranks exchange the new positions with `MPI_Allgatherv`, staged through pinned host memory. With a single rank the exchange is skipped and the copy stays on the GPU. At the end, velocities are gathered to rank 0 for `-r` and `-v`. Only rank 0 prints, and every rank returns the same exit code.\n- **CUDA:** one kernel per step computes the forces and moves the bodies for that rank's block. Each thread handles one body and reads other positions in tiles from shared memory. The block size adapts so small problems still keep the GPU busy, and ranks with no bodies are handled.\n- **OpenMP:** handles the host-side work: packing the data, the validation scan, and the final-energy calculation. The energy is also spread across MPI ranks by row, so the triangular loop stays balanced.\n\n**Correctness**\n- As in the original, every step uses the positions from the start of that step, and each body's forces are summed in the original j order.\n- I read the assembly of the original build and found which multiply-adds GCC fuses (`-O3 -march=native`). The kernel repeats that exact rounding sequence using CUDA's explicit-rounding functions.\n- Result hashes match the original for n = 3, 777, 1000, 1024 and 4096, on 1 and 4 ranks; `-s 0` also matches.\n- **Exception:** the \"Final energy\" printed by `-v` can differ in the last printed digit (e.g. …032543 vs …032545). That's because the parallel sum adds the terms in a different order. The validation itself passes.\n\n**Performance** (RTX 3090s, 10 steps, single-GPU times are this version's)\n\n| Bodies | Original CPU | 1 GPU | 2 GPUs | 4 GPUs |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 4096 | 1239 ms | 27 ms | – | 29 ms |\n| 16384 | – | 352 ms | 195 ms | 128 ms |\n| 65536 | – | 5144 ms | 2620 ms | 1350 ms |\n| 131072 | – | 17985 ms | 10422 ms | 5270 ms |\n\nThe 4096-body case doesn't gain from extra GPUs at this size. The main limit is double precision, which is slow on 3090s, plus the exactly-rounded square root and division. A single-precision or approximate inverse-square-root version would be much faster, but its results would no longer match the original. I kept exact matching.\n\n**Build notes (`CMakeLists.txt`)**\n- `nbody.cpp` is compiled by nvcc, and the build links MPI, OpenMP and the CUDA runtime, none of which are new dependencies.\n- It now requires CMake 3.24 or later.\n- The GPU architecture defaults to whatever card is in the build machine. Set `CMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES` if you build on a machine without one.\n- nvcc 12.0 doesn't support GCC 13, so the build uses `g++-12` as nvcc's host compiler when it's installed. Its output is bit-for-bit identical to the original program's. The executable is still `nbody` and is launched with `mpirun -np <ranks> ./nbody ...`.\n\n**How the work is split**\n- **MPI:** bodies are divided into equal contiguous blocks, one rank per GPU. Each rank picks its GPU from its position among the ranks on its node. After every step the ranks exchange the new positions with `MPI_Allgatherv`, staged through pinned host memory. With a single rank the exchange is skipped and the copy stays on the GPU. At the end, velocities are gathered to rank 0 for `-r` and `-v`. Only rank 0 prints, and every rank returns the same exit code.\n- **CUDA:** one kernel per step computes the forces and moves the bodies for that rank's block. Each thread handles one body and reads other positions in tiles from shared memory. The block size adapts so small problems still keep the GPU busy, and ranks with no bodies are handled.\n- **OpenMP:** handles the host-side work: packing the data, the validation scan, and the final-energy calculation. The energy is also spread across MPI ranks by row, so the triangular loop stays balanced.\n\n**Correctness**\n- As in the original, every step uses the positions from the start of that step, and each body's forces are summed in the original j order.\n- I read the assembly of the original build and found which multiply-adds GCC fuses (`-O3 -march=native`). The kernel repeats that exact rounding sequence using CUDA's explicit-rounding functions.\n- Result hashes match the original for n = 3, 777, 1000, 1024 and 4096, on 1 and 4 ranks; `-s 0` also matches.\n- **Exception:** the \"Final energy\" printed by `-v` can differ in the last printed digit (e.g. …032543 vs …032545). That's because the parallel sum adds the terms in a different order. The validation itself passes.\n\n**Performance** (RTX 3090s, 10 steps, single-GPU times are this version's)\n\n| Bodies | Original CPU | 1 GPU | 2 GPUs | 4 GPUs |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 4096 | 1239 ms | 27 ms | – | 29 ms |\n| 16384 | – | 352 ms | 195 ms | 128 ms |\n| 65536 | – | 5144 ms | 2620 ms | 1350 ms |\n| 131072 | – | 17985 ms | 10422 ms | 5270 ms |\n\nThe 4096-body case doesn't gain from extra GPUs at this size. The main limit is double precision, which is slow on 3090s, plus the exactly-rounded square root and division. A single-precision or approximate inverse-square-root version would be much faster, but its results would no longer match the original. I kept exact matching.\n\n**Build notes (`CMakeLists.txt`)**\n- `nbody.cpp` is compiled by nvcc, and the build links MPI, OpenMP and the CUDA runtime, none of which are new dependencies.\n- It now requires CMake 3.24 or later.\n- The GPU architecture defaults to whatever card is in the build machine. Set `CMAKE_CUDA_ARCHITECTURES` if you build on a machine without one.\n- nvcc 12.0 doesn't support GCC 13, so the build uses `g++-12` as nvcc's host compiler when it's installed.
